14
Algorithm: EDs from Demand-Size-Large Sparse Cuts

We now show how to compute expander decompositions from demand-size large length-constrained sparse cuts. Specifically, we show the following. Recall the definition of an
\alpha
-approximate
(\leq h,s)
-length
\phi
-sparse cut with length approximation
\alpha_{s}
and sparsity approximation
\alpha_{\phi}
from
Definition

13.3
.

Lemma 14.1
.

Fix
\alpha,\alpha_{\phi},\alpha_{s}>1
and let
\textnormal{{W}}_{\textnormal{sparse-cut}}(A,m)
be the time to compute an
\alpha
-approximate
(\leq h^{\prime},s^{\prime})
-length
\phi^{\prime}
-sparse cut with sparsity approximation
\alpha_{\phi}
and length approximation
\alpha_{s}
w.r.t. node-weighting
A
in a graph with
m^{\prime}\leq m
edges for
h^{\prime}\leq h
,
s^{\prime}\leq s
and
\phi^{\prime}\geq\phi
.

Then, for every
\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}>0
, one can compute a
(\leq h,s)
-length
\phi
-expander decomposition for
A
with cut slack

\displaystyle\kappa=\tilde{O}\left(\alpha\cdot N^{O(\epsilon^{\prime})}\right)\cdot\tilde{O}\left(\alpha_{\phi}\cdot s\cdot N^{O(1/\sqrt{s})}\ \right)^{O(1/\epsilon^{\prime})},

length slack

\displaystyle s=\alpha_{s}^{O(1/\epsilon^{\prime})},

work

\displaystyle\textnormal{{W}}_{\textnormal{ED}}(A,m)\leq\tilde{O}\left(\frac{N^{O(\epsilon^{\prime})}}{\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\alpha\right)\cdot\textnormal{{W}}_{\textnormal{sparse-cut}}(A,m)

and depth

\displaystyle\textnormal{{D}}_{\textnormal{ED}}(A,m)\leq\tilde{O}\left(\frac{N^{O(\epsilon^{\prime})}}{\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\alpha\right)\cdot\textnormal{{D}}_{\textnormal{sparse-cut}}(A,m).

We begin by describing the algorithm we use to prove the above. We give pseudo-code in
Algorithm

2
. The algorithm runs in
\frac{1}{\epsilon^{\prime}}
top-level iterations we call epochs. In epoch
\mathsf{epoch}
the algorithm repeatedly cuts an
\alpha
-approximate demand-size-largest
(\leq h_{\mathsf{epoch}},s_{\mathsf{epoch}})
-length
\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}}
-sparse cut for the target node weighting
A
in the current graph with sparsity slack
\alpha_{\phi}
and length slack
\alpha_{s}
. For the next epoch the algorithm adjusts its target values for sparsity and length by decreasing
\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}}
by about
1/\alpha_{\phi}
,
h_{\mathsf{epoch}}
by about
1/\alpha_{s}
and increasing
s_{\mathsf{epoch}}
by about
\alpha_{s}
.

The proof shows that, at the end of an epoch
\mathsf{epoch}
, no demand-size-large
(h_{\mathsf{epoch}}^{\prime},s_{\mathsf{epoch}}^{\prime})
-length
\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}}^{\prime}
-sparse cuts exist for
A
anymore for
h_{\mathsf{epoch}}^{\prime}
,
s_{\mathsf{epoch}}^{\prime}
and
\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}}
each slightly more relaxed than
h_{\mathsf{epoch}}
,
s_{\mathsf{epoch}}
and
\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}}
. More specifically, for
h_{\mathsf{epoch}}^{\prime}\leq h_{\mathsf{epoch}}/\alpha_{s}
,
s_{\mathsf{epoch}}^{\prime}=s_{\mathsf{epoch}}\cdot\alpha_{s}
and
\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}}^{\prime}=\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}}/\alpha_{\phi}
. Initially, no such cut of demand-size strictly more than
|A|
exists (trivially) and in each epoch we improve the quality of this upper bound on the demand-size-largest cut by an
N^{\epsilon^{\prime}}
factor so that after
1/\epsilon^{\prime}
iterations no such cut exists (for appropriately relaxed length, length slack and sparsity). Generally speaking, the trick is to make sure that our upper bound on
|A|
improves faster than we must relax our length, length slack and sparsity.

Algorithm 2

Length-Constrained Expander Decompositions (from Large Sparse Cuts)

Input:
Edge-capacitated graph
G_{0}
, parameters
\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}\in(0,1)
, node-weighting
A
on
G
, length bound
h
, and conductance bound
\phi>0
, an algorithm for demand-size-largest sparse cut.

Output:

(\leq h,s)
-length
\phi
-expander decomposition

Initialize Graph and Number of Iterations:

G=G_{0}
and
l=\Theta(\log N\cdot\alpha\cdot N^{O(\epsilon^{\prime})})

Initialize Length:

h_{0}=\alpha_{s}\cdot(2\alpha_{s})^{1/\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot h

Initialize Length Slack:

s_{0}=2\cdot\alpha_{s}^{1/\epsilon^{\prime}}

Initialize Sparsity:

\phi_{0}=\phi\cdot\alpha_{\phi}\cdot\prod_{\mathsf{epoch}\in[1/\epsilon^{\prime}]}\left(\tilde{O}(\alpha_{\phi}\cdot s_{\mathsf{epoch}}^{3}\cdot N^{O(1/s_{\mathsf{epoch}})})\right)

for

\mathsf{epoch}=1,2,\ldots 1/\epsilon^{\prime}

do

Update Length:

h_{\mathsf{epoch}}=\frac{1}{2\alpha_{s}}\cdot h_{\mathsf{epoch}-1}

Update Length Slack:

s_{\mathsf{epoch}}=\alpha_{s}\cdot s_{\mathsf{epoch}-1}

Update Sparsity:

\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}}=\frac{1}{\tilde{O}(\alpha_{\phi}\cdot s_{\mathsf{epoch}}^{3}\cdot N^{O(1/s_{\mathsf{epoch}})})}\cdot\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}-1}

for

j=1,2,\ldots,l

do

Let
C
be an
\alpha
-approximate demand-size-largest
(\leq h_{\mathsf{epoch}},s_{\mathsf{epoch}})
-length
\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}}
-sparse cut

for
A
and length
h_{\mathsf{epoch}}^{\prime\prime}
in
G
with length and sparsity approximation
\alpha_{s}
and
\alpha_{\phi}
.

Update Graph:

G=G+s_{\mathsf{epoch}}\cdot h^{\prime\prime}_{\mathsf{epoch}}\cdot C
.

return

\sum_{C}C

We will use the following notion of the demand-size of a sequence of cuts and the subsequent relation. Below, recall the definition of
LDSC
from
Definition

13.2
.

Definition 14.2

(
LDSCS
)
.

Fix a graph
G
, node-weighting
A
and parameters
h
,
s
and
\phi
.
LDSCS
is the demand-size of the demand-size-largest sequence of
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-sparse moving cuts. Specifically,

\displaystyle\text{LDSCS}(\phi,h,s):=\sum_{i}A_{(h,s)}(C_{i})

where above
A_{(h,s)}(C_{i})
is computed after applying all
C_{j}
for
j<i
and
(C_{1},C_{2},\ldots)
is the
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-expanding moving cut sequence maximizing
\sum_{i}A_{(h,s)}(C_{i})
.

Lemma 14.3
.

Given graph
G
and node-weighting
A
, we have that

\displaystyle\text{LDSCS}(\phi,h,s)\leq\text{LDSC}(\phi^{\prime},h^{\prime},s^{\prime})

where
h^{\prime}=2h
,
s^{\prime}=\frac{(s-2)}{2}
and
\phi^{\prime}=s^{3}\cdot\log^{3}N\cdot N^{O(1/s)}\cdot\phi
.

Proof.

Let
(C_{1},C_{2},\ldots)
be a sequence of
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-sparse cuts such that
\sum_{i}A_{(h,s)}(C_{i})=\text{LDSCS}(\phi,h,s)
where
\phi_{i}\leq\phi
is the minimum value for which
C_{i}
is
\phi_{i}
-sparse. Let
D_{1},D_{2},\ldots
be the demands witnessing these cuts so that for all
i
we have

\displaystyle|C_{i}|/\phi=|D_{i}|.

Let
C=\sum_{i}C_{i}
. By
Theorem

1.2
we know that
C
is an
(h^{\prime},s^{\prime})
-length
\phi^{\prime}
-sparse cut for
A
and so there must be some
h^{\prime}
-length demand
D
which is
h^{\prime}s^{\prime}
-separated by
C
and
D
has size at least

\displaystyle\frac{|C|}{\phi^{\prime}}=|C|\frac{\sum_{i}|C_{i}|/\phi_{i}}{\sum_{i}|C_{i}|}=\sum_{i}|D_{i}|

In other words, the demand-size of
C
is at least
\sum_{i}|D_{i}|
, as required.
∎

Lemma 14.4
.

At the end of the
\mathsf{epoch}
th epoch of
Algorithm

2
, we have that for every
h^{\prime}_{\mathsf{epoch}}\leq h_{\mathsf{epoch}}/\alpha_{s}
that the demand-size-largest
(h^{\prime}_{\mathsf{epoch}},s_{\mathsf{epoch}})
-length
(\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}}/\alpha_{\phi})
-sparse cut has
(h^{\prime}_{\mathsf{epoch}},s_{\mathsf{epoch}}\cdot\alpha_{s})
-length demand-size at most
|A|\cdot\left(\frac{1}{N^{O(\epsilon^{\prime})}}\right)^{\mathsf{epoch}}
. In other words, we have for every
h^{\prime}_{\mathsf{epoch}}\leq h_{\mathsf{epoch}}/\alpha_{s}
that

\displaystyle\text{LDSC}(\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}}/\alpha_{\phi},h^{\prime}_{\mathsf{epoch}},s_{\mathsf{epoch}}\cdot\alpha_{s})\leq|A|\cdot\left(\frac{1}{N^{O(\epsilon^{\prime})}}\right)^{\mathsf{epoch}}.

Proof.

We prove this by induction. Let
\gamma:=1/N^{O(\epsilon^{\prime})}
for convenience of notation. The base case when
\mathsf{epoch}=0
is trivial as the demand-size of any cut is trivially at most
|A|
. Next, suppose
\mathsf{epoch}>0
and assume for the sake of contradiction that our inductive hypothesis does not hold and so there is some
\hat{h}_{\mathsf{epoch}}\leq h_{\mathsf{epoch}}/\alpha_{s}
such that at the end of the
\mathsf{epoch}
th epoch we have

\displaystyle\text{LDSC}(\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}}/\alpha_{\phi},\hat{h}_{\mathsf{epoch}},s_{\mathsf{epoch}}\cdot\alpha_{s})>|A|/\gamma^{\mathsf{epoch}}.

(43)

Consider a cut
C
we compute in an epoch,
\mathsf{epoch}
, with corresponding length
h^{\prime\prime}_{\mathsf{epoch}}
.
Unpacking the definition of approximate demand-size largest sparse cuts (
Definition

13.3
), we have that
C
is an
(h^{\prime\prime}_{\mathsf{epoch}},s_{\mathsf{epoch}})
-length
\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}}
-sparse cut for
A
where
h^{\prime\prime}_{\mathsf{epoch}}\leq\alpha_{s}\cdot h_{\mathsf{epoch}}
and, for any
h^{\prime}_{\mathsf{epoch}}\leq h_{\mathsf{epoch}}/\alpha_{s}
and, in particular, for
\hat{h}_{\mathsf{epoch}}
we have

\displaystyle A_{(h^{\prime\prime}_{\mathsf{epoch}},s_{\mathsf{epoch}})}(C)\geq\frac{1}{\alpha}\cdot\text{LDSC}(\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}}/\alpha_{\phi},\hat{h}_{\mathsf{epoch}},s_{\mathsf{epoch}}\cdot\alpha_{s})

at the moment when
C
is computed. Combining the above with
Equation

43
and the fact that
\text{LDSC}(\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}}/\alpha_{\phi},\hat{h}_{\mathsf{epoch}},s_{\mathsf{epoch}}\cdot\alpha_{s})
can only be smaller at the end of our epoch than in the middle of it , we get

\displaystyle A_{(h^{\prime\prime}_{\mathsf{epoch}},s_{\mathsf{epoch}})}(C)>\frac{1}{\alpha}\cdot|A|/\gamma^{\mathsf{epoch}}

(44)

Let
h^{\prime\prime}_{\mathsf{epoch}}\leq h_{\mathsf{epoch}}\cdot\alpha_{s}
be a value that could correspond to a cut in the
\mathsf{epoch}
th iteration. Notice that, by our definition
h_{\mathsf{epoch}}=\frac{1}{2\alpha_{s}}\cdot h_{\mathsf{epoch}-1}
, we have that

\displaystyle 2h^{\prime\prime}_{\mathsf{epoch}}\leq 2\alpha_{s}\cdot h_{\mathsf{epoch}}=h_{\mathsf{epoch}-1}.

(45)

We may assume these values are, without loss of generality, powers of
2
and so after we compute
\Theta(\log N\cdot\alpha\cdot\gamma)
-many cuts, we know that there must be some
h^{\prime\prime}_{\mathsf{epoch}}
such that
\mathcal{C}_{h^{\prime\prime}_{\mathsf{epoch}}}
contains at least
(\alpha\cdot\gamma)
-many cuts. We let
(C_{1},C_{2},\ldots)
be these cuts. Applying
Equation

44
to each of our
C_{i}
s, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{i}A_{(h^{\prime\prime}_{\mathsf{epoch}},s_{\mathsf{epoch}})}(C_{i})>|A|/\gamma^{\mathsf{epoch}-1}.

(46)

(C_{1},C_{2},\ldots)
is an
(h_{\mathsf{epoch}}^{\prime\prime},s_{\mathsf{epoch}})
-length
\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}}
-sparse sequence of moving cuts and so forms a candidate for the demand-size-largest such sequence.

It therefore follows that at the end of the
\mathsf{epoch}
th epoch we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i}A_{(h^{\prime\prime}_{\mathsf{epoch}},s_{\mathsf{epoch}})}(C_{i})

\displaystyle\leq\text{LDSCS}(\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}},h^{\prime\prime}_{\mathsf{epoch}},s_{\mathsf{epoch}})

\displaystyle\leq\text{LDSC}\left(\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}}\cdot\tilde{O}(s_{\mathsf{epoch}}^{3}\cdot N^{O(1/s_{\mathsf{epoch}})}),2h^{\prime\prime}_{\mathsf{epoch}},s_{\mathsf{epoch}}\right)

\displaystyle=\text{LDSC}\left(\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}-1},2h^{\prime\prime}_{\mathsf{epoch}},s_{\mathsf{epoch}-1}\cdot\alpha_{s}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\text{LDSC}\left(\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}-1},h_{\mathsf{epoch}-1},s_{\mathsf{epoch}-1}\right)

\displaystyle\leq|A|/\gamma^{\mathsf{epoch}-1}.

where, above, the second inequality follows from
Lemma

14.3
, the third from the definition of
\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}-1}
and
s_{\mathsf{epoch}-1}
, the fourth from
Equation

45
and the fact that
\text{LDSC}(\phi,h,s)
is monotone increasing in
h
(as long as
s\geq 2
) and monotone decreasing in
s
and the fifth from our inductive hypothesis. However, the above contradicts
Equation

46
.
∎

We conclude with our proof of
Lemma

14.1
.
See
14.1

Proof.

We use
Algorithm

2
.

First, we claim that the returned cuts
\sum_{C}C
are indeed an
(\leq h,s)
-length expander decomposition for sparsity
\phi
with cut slack
\kappa
.

Let
\mathsf{epoch}
be
1/\epsilon^{\prime}
. By our choice of how we initialize
h_{0}
,
\phi_{0}
, observe that after our
1/\epsilon^{\prime}
-many epochs we have that
h_{\mathsf{epoch}}=\alpha_{s}\cdot h
,
s_{\mathsf{epoch}}=2\cdot\alpha_{s}^{2/\epsilon^{\prime}}
and
\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}}=\alpha_{\phi}\cdot\phi
. Letting
s=2\cdot\alpha_{s}^{2/\epsilon^{\prime}}
and applying
Lemma

14.4
to the final epoch of our algorithm, we therefore have that, after applying
\sum_{C}C
to our graph, for every
h^{\prime}\leq h_{\mathsf{epoch}}/\alpha_{s}\leq h
that the
(h,s)
-length demand-size of the demand-size-largest
(h,s)
-length
\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}}/\alpha_{\phi}=\phi
-sparse cut is at most

\displaystyle|A|\cdot\left(\frac{1}{N^{O(\epsilon^{\prime})}}^{1/\epsilon^{\prime}}\right)\leq m\cdot\left(\frac{1}{N^{O(\epsilon^{\prime})}}\right)^{1/\epsilon^{\prime}}<1

where, above, we applied the fact that
|A|\leq m
(since it must be degree-respecting). Since the demand-size of a cut is integral, it follows that for all
h^{\prime}\leq h
no
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-sparse cut exists and so
\sum_{C}C
is indeed a
(\leq h,s)
-length
\phi
-expander decomposition (
Definition

5.20
).

We next consider the cut slack of this expander decomposition. Observe that each cut
C
that we compute in the epoch
\mathsf{epoch}
is, by construction, a (
(\leq h_{\mathsf{epoch}},s_{\mathsf{epoch}})
-length)
\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}}
-sparse cut for
|A|
and so has size at most

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}}\cdot|A|

\displaystyle\leq\phi\cdot\alpha_{\phi}\cdot\left(\tilde{O}(\alpha_{\phi}\cdot s_{\mathsf{epoch}}^{3}\cdot N^{O(1/s_{\mathsf{epoch}})})\right)^{1/\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot|A|

\displaystyle\leq\phi\cdot\alpha_{\phi}\cdot\left(\tilde{O}(\alpha_{\phi}\cdot s^{3}\cdot N^{O(1/s_{0})})\right)^{1/\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot|A|

\displaystyle\leq\phi\cdot\alpha_{\phi}^{O(1/\epsilon^{\prime})}\cdot s^{O(1/\epsilon^{\prime})}\cdot N^{O(1/(\epsilon^{\prime}\cdot\sqrt{s}))}\cdot\left(\tilde{O}(1)\right)^{1/\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot|A|

Furthermore, applying the fact that, although we compute
l=\tilde{O}(\alpha\cdot N^{O(\epsilon^{\prime})})
total such cuts in one iteration, the size of these cuts is geometrically increasing, so the entire size of
\sum_{C}C
is dominated by the sum of the cuts we compute in the last iteration. Namely, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{C}|C|\leq\phi|A|\cdot\left(\tilde{O}\left(\alpha\cdot N^{O(\epsilon^{\prime})}\right)\cdot\alpha_{\phi}^{O(1/\epsilon^{\prime})}\cdot s^{O(1/\epsilon^{\prime})}\cdot N^{O(1/(\epsilon^{\prime}\cdot\sqrt{s}))}\cdot\left(\tilde{O}(1)\right)^{1/\epsilon^{\prime}}\right)

giving our bound on the cut slack.

Lastly, the work and depth of our algorithm is trivial since we compute
\tilde{O}(\frac{N^{O(\epsilon^{\prime})}}{\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\alpha)
-many cuts.

∎

15
Algorithm: Length-Constrained EDs from “The Spiral”

We conclude by combining our algorithm which computes length-constrained expander decompositions using demand-size-large length-constrained sparse cuts (
Lemma

14.1
) with our algorithm that computes large length-constrained sparse cuts using length-constrained expander decompositions (
Lemma

13.4
). This forms a “spiral” of mutual recursion where each time we go around the spiral we make substantial progress on the size of the problem on which we are working (in terms of node-weighting size). We first state (and prove) our result with a maximally general tradeoff of parameters (
\epsilon
and
\epsilon^{\prime}
). We next simplify this presentation by choosing these parameters to get our final theorem.

Theorem 15.1
.

There exists an algorithm that, given edge-capacitated graph
G
, parameters
\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}\in(0,1)
, node-weighting
A
on
G
, length bound

h
, and conductance bound
\phi>0
, computes an
(\leq h,s)
-length witnessed
\phi
-expander
decomposition for
A
in
G

with cut and length slack

\displaystyle\kappa=N^{O(\epsilon^{\prime})}\cdot\tilde{O}\left(\frac{N^{O(\epsilon)}}{\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon)}\right)^{O(1/\epsilon^{\prime})}\qquad\qquad s=(1/\epsilon)^{O(1/\epsilon^{\prime})}

with work

\displaystyle\textnormal{{W}}_{\textnormal{ED}}(A,m)\leq m\cdot\tilde{O}\left(\frac{N^{O(\epsilon+\epsilon^{\prime})}}{\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}\right)\left(\tilde{O}(1/\epsilon)^{O(1/(\epsilon\epsilon^{\prime}))}+\operatorname{poly}(h)\right).

and depth

\displaystyle\textnormal{{D}}_{\textnormal{ED}}(A,m)\leq\tilde{O}\left(\frac{N^{O(\epsilon+\epsilon^{\prime})}}{\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}\right)\left(\tilde{O}(1/\epsilon)^{O(1/(\epsilon\epsilon^{\prime}))}+\operatorname{poly}(h)\right).

Proof.

Applying
Lemma

13.4
with
L=N^{\epsilon^{\prime}}
we have that one can compute an
\alpha
-approximate
(\leq h,s)
-length demand-size largest cut with sparsity and length approximation
\alpha_{\phi}
and
\alpha_{s}
where

\displaystyle\alpha=\tilde{O}\left(\frac{N^{O(\epsilon)}}{\epsilon^{3}}\right)\qquad\qquad\alpha_{\phi}=\frac{s^{3}N^{O(1/s)}}{\epsilon}\qquad\qquad\alpha_{s}=\max\left(2,\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\cdot(s)^{1+O(1/\epsilon)}\right)

with work at most

\displaystyle\textnormal{{W}}_{\textnormal{sparse-cut}}(A,m)\leq m\cdot\tilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\cdot(s)^{O(1/\epsilon)}\cdot N^{O(\epsilon)}+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\cdot N^{O(\epsilon+\epsilon^{\prime})}\cdot\operatorname{poly}(h)\right)+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\sum_{i}\textnormal{{W}}_{\textnormal{ED}}(A_{i},m_{i})

where
|A_{i}|\leq\frac{|A|}{N^{\epsilon^{\prime}}}
for all
i
and
\{m_{i}\}_{i}
are non-negative integers satisfying

\displaystyle\sum_{i}m_{i}\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon}\cdot\tilde{O}(m+n^{1+O(\epsilon)}+N^{O(\epsilon)}).

(47)

Likewise, applying
Lemma

14.1
, we have that we can compute a
(\leq h,s)
-length
\phi
-expander decomposition for
A
with cut slack

\displaystyle\kappa=\tilde{O}\left(\alpha\cdot N^{O(\epsilon^{\prime})}\right)\cdot\tilde{O}\left(\alpha_{\phi}\cdot s\cdot N^{O(1/\sqrt{s})}\ \right)^{O(1/\epsilon^{\prime})},

length slack

\displaystyle s=\alpha_{s}^{O(1/\epsilon^{\prime})},

with work

\displaystyle\textnormal{{W}}_{\textnormal{ED}}(A,m)\leq\tilde{O}\left(\frac{N^{O(\epsilon^{\prime})}}{\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\alpha\right)\cdot\textnormal{{W}}_{\textnormal{sparse-cut}}(A,m)

where
\alpha
,
\alpha_{\phi}
and
\alpha_{s}
are the approximation factors of our approximate demand-size-largest sparse cut algorithm.

We now combine the above recursions into a single recursion to compute a
(\leq h,s_{i})
-length
\phi
-expander decomposition for
A
. We will index each level of our recursion by
i
. Observe that at the
i
th level of our recursion, we have that

\displaystyle s_{i}=\alpha_{s,i}^{O(1/\epsilon^{\prime})}=\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\cdot s_{i-1}^{1+O(1/\epsilon)}

Furthermore, since each time we recurse we reduce our node-weighting’s size by a multiplicative
N^{\epsilon^{\prime}}
, the depth of our recursion is at most
1/\epsilon^{\prime}
and so we have that
s_{i}
is always at most

\displaystyle s_{i}\leq\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right)^{O(1/\epsilon^{\prime})}

It follows that our cut slack in the
i
th level of our recursion is

\displaystyle\kappa_{i}

\displaystyle=\tilde{O}\left(\alpha\cdot N^{O(\epsilon^{\prime})}\right)\cdot\tilde{O}\left(\alpha_{\phi}\cdot s_{i}\cdot N^{O(1/\sqrt{s_{i}})}\ \right)^{O(1/\epsilon^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\tilde{O}\left(\frac{N^{O(\epsilon+\epsilon^{\prime})}}{\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon)}\right)\cdot\tilde{O}\left(\frac{s_{i}^{3}N^{O(1/s_{i})}}{\epsilon}\cdot s_{i}\cdot N^{O(1/\sqrt{s_{i}})}\ \right)^{O(1/\epsilon^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\tilde{O}\left(\frac{N^{O(\epsilon+\epsilon^{\prime})}}{\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon)}\right)\cdot\tilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon)}\cdot N^{O(1/s_{i}+1/\sqrt{s_{i}})}\right)^{O(1/\epsilon^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=N^{O(\epsilon^{\prime})}\cdot\tilde{O}\left(\frac{N^{O(\epsilon)}}{\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon)}\right)^{O(1/\epsilon^{\prime})}.

where in the last line we used the fact that
1/\sqrt{s_{i}}+1/s_{i}\geq 1/\epsilon
for every value of
s_{i}
we consider.

We next bound the work of our algorithm. Letting
m_{j}
be the total number of edges at the
j
th level of our recursion and applying our bound on
\sum_{i}m_{i}
from
Equation

47
we have

\displaystyle m_{j+1}\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon}\cdot\tilde{O}(m_{j}+n^{1+O(\epsilon)}+N^{O(\epsilon)})

Applying the fact that our recurrence has depth at most
1/\epsilon^{\prime}
, we get that the maximum number of edges across an entire level of recursion
j
is at most

\displaystyle m_{j}

\displaystyle\leq m\cdot\tilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right)^{1/\epsilon^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{\epsilon^{\prime}}\left(n^{1+O(\epsilon)}+N^{O(\epsilon)}\right)

\displaystyle=m\cdot\tilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right)^{1/\epsilon^{\prime}}+\frac{N^{O(\epsilon)}}{\epsilon^{\prime}}

(48)

where, above,
m
is the number of edges in our original graph. On the other hand, we have that (apart from the recursive calls), the entire work of our algorithm at a single level of recursion is at most

\displaystyle m_{j}\cdot\tilde{O}\left(\frac{\alpha\cdot N^{O(\epsilon^{\prime})}}{\epsilon^{\prime}}\right)\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\cdot(s)^{O(1/\epsilon)}\cdot N^{O(\epsilon)}+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\cdot N^{O(\epsilon+\epsilon^{\prime})}\cdot\operatorname{poly}(h)\right)

\displaystyle\leq m_{j}\cdot\tilde{O}\left(\frac{N^{O(\epsilon+\epsilon^{\prime})}}{\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}\right)\left((s)^{O(1/\epsilon)}+\operatorname{poly}(h)\right)

\displaystyle\leq m_{j}\cdot\tilde{O}\left(\frac{N^{O(\epsilon+\epsilon^{\prime})}}{\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}\right)\left((1/\epsilon)^{O(1/(\epsilon\epsilon^{\prime}))}+\operatorname{poly}(h)\right)

(49)

Combining
48
and
49
we get that the total work on a single level of recursion (again, excluding recursive calls) is at most

\displaystyle\left(m\cdot\tilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right)^{1/\epsilon^{\prime}}+\frac{N^{O(\epsilon)}}{\epsilon^{\prime}}\right)\cdot\tilde{O}\left(\frac{N^{O(\epsilon+\epsilon^{\prime})}}{\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}\right)\left((1/\epsilon)^{O(1/(\epsilon\epsilon^{\prime}))}+\operatorname{poly}(h)\right)

\displaystyle=m\cdot\tilde{O}\left(\frac{N^{O(\epsilon+\epsilon^{\prime})}}{\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}\right)\left(\tilde{O}(1/\epsilon)^{O(1/(\epsilon\epsilon^{\prime}))}+\operatorname{poly}(h)\right)

Summing over our
1/\epsilon^{\prime}
-many recursive levels of our algorithm then gives our work bound. The argument for depth is analogous (and, in fact, easier because we do not have to control the total number of edges over each level of recursion using
Equation

47
).

∎

Lastly, we give the simplified version of the above theorem with witnesses.

See
1.1

Proof.

We first apply
Theorem

15.1
with
\epsilon^{\prime}_{0}=\epsilon
and
\epsilon_{0}=\epsilon^{2}
where
\epsilon^{\prime}_{0}
and
\epsilon_{0}
are the parameters described in
Theorem

15.1
. Likewise, let
c
be an upper-bound on the exponent of the poly-log in the
\tilde{O}
notations and the exponent of all
\operatorname{poly}
notation of
Theorem

15.1
. It follows that we can compute a
(\leq h,s)
-length
\phi
-expander decomposition for
A
in
G
with cut and length slack

\displaystyle\kappa=\frac{N^{O(\epsilon)}}{(\epsilon)^{c\cdot O(1/\epsilon)}}\cdot\log^{c\cdot O(1/\epsilon)}N\qquad\qquad s=(1/\epsilon^{2})^{O(1/\epsilon)}

with work

\displaystyle\textnormal{{W}}_{\textnormal{ED}}(A,m)\leq m\cdot\tilde{O}\left(\frac{N^{O(\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon))}}{\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon)}\right)\left((1/\epsilon)^{O(1/\epsilon^{3})}\cdot\log^{c\cdot O(1/\epsilon)}N+\operatorname{poly}(h)\right).

and depth

\displaystyle\textnormal{{D}}_{\textnormal{ED}}(A,m)\leq\tilde{O}\left(\frac{N^{O(\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon))}}{\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon)}\right)\left((1/\epsilon)^{O(1/\epsilon^{3})}\cdot\log^{c\cdot O(1/\epsilon)}N+\operatorname{poly}(h)\right).

We begin by reasoning about our cut slack. Notice that, for a suitable large hidden constant in the
\Omega
we have that if

\displaystyle\epsilon\geq\Omega\left(\sqrt{\frac{c\cdot\log\log n}{\log N}}\right)

then we have that

\displaystyle\frac{c\cdot O(1/\epsilon)\cdot\log\log N}{\log N}\leq\epsilon

(50)

Similarly, we have that if

\displaystyle\epsilon\geq\Omega\left(\left({\frac{c}{\log n}}\right)^{1/3}\right)

then

\displaystyle\frac{\log(1/\epsilon)\cdot c\cdot O(1/\epsilon)}{\log N}\leq\frac{c\cdot O(1/\epsilon^{2})}{\log N}\leq\epsilon

Thus, our cut slack is

\displaystyle\kappa=N^{O(\epsilon)+\frac{c\cdot O(1/\epsilon)\cdot\log\log N}{\log N}+\frac{\log(1/\epsilon)\cdot c\cdot O(1/\epsilon)}{\log N}}\leq N^{O(\epsilon)}

Likewise, our length slack is

\displaystyle s=(1/\epsilon^{2})^{O(1/\epsilon)}=\exp(O(1/\epsilon)\cdot\log(1/\epsilon^{2}))\leq\exp(O(1/\epsilon^{2}))=\exp(\operatorname{poly}(1/\epsilon)).

Lastly, observe that if

\displaystyle\epsilon\geq\Omega\left(\frac{1}{\log N}\right)^{(1/5)}

and

\displaystyle\epsilon\geq\Omega\left(\log\log n/\log n\right)

then we have

\displaystyle\frac{O(1/\epsilon^{3})\cdot\log(1/\epsilon)}{\log N}+\frac{c\cdot O(1/\epsilon)\cdot\log\log N}{\log N}\leq\frac{O(1/\epsilon^{4})}{\log N}+\frac{O(1/\epsilon)\cdot\log\log N}{\log N}\leq O(\epsilon)

and so our work is

\displaystyle\textnormal{{W}}_{\textnormal{ED}}(A,m)

\displaystyle\leq m\cdot\tilde{O}\left(\frac{N^{O(\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon))}}{\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon)}\right)\left((1/\epsilon)^{O(1/\epsilon^{3})}\cdot\log^{c\cdot O(1/\epsilon)}N+\operatorname{poly}(h)\right)

\displaystyle=m\cdot\tilde{O}\left(\frac{N^{O(\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon))}}{\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon)}\right)\left(N^{\frac{O(1/\epsilon^{3})\cdot\log(1/\epsilon)}{\log N}+\frac{c\cdot O(1/\epsilon)\cdot\log\log N}{\log N}}+\operatorname{poly}(h)\right)

\displaystyle=m\cdot\tilde{O}\left(\frac{N^{O(\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon))}}{\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon)}\right)\left(N^{O(\epsilon)}+\operatorname{poly}(h)\right)

\displaystyle=m\cdot\tilde{O}\left(\frac{N^{O(\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon))}}{\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon)}\right)\cdot\operatorname{poly}(h)

\displaystyle\leq m\cdot\tilde{O}\left(N^{O(\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon))}\cdot\operatorname{poly}(h)\right)

Where in the last line we used the fact that
\epsilon\geq 1/\log^{O(1)}N
. The final result comes from letting
\epsilon
above be smaller by a suitable large constant (to get the cut slack from
N^{O(\epsilon)}
to
n^{\epsilon}
). Our depth bound is symmetric.

Lastly, we discuss how to compute our witness. The basic idea is to slightly strengthen the expander decomposition we compute to deal with the slacks from
Lemma

13.4
. In particular, recall that by
Lemma

13.4
if the graph is already
(\leq h\cdot\frac{1}{\epsilon}\cdot s^{O(1/\epsilon)},s)
-length
\phi
-expanding then when we apply
Lemma

13.4
we get a
(\leq h,s_{w})
-length
\phi_{w}
-expansion witness where
s_{w}=\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\cdot s^{O(1/\epsilon)}
and
\phi_{w}=\tilde{O}(\phi\epsilon/N^{O(\epsilon)})
.

Fix an
\epsilon
and let
\epsilon_{0}=\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon)
so that if
s_{0}=\exp(\operatorname{poly}(1/\epsilon_{0}))
we have that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\epsilon_{0}^{2}}\cdot s_{0}^{O(1/\epsilon_{0})}\leq\exp(\operatorname{poly}(1/\epsilon))

and we let
\phi_{0}=\phi\cdot\tilde{O}\left(N^{O(\epsilon_{0})}\right)/\epsilon_{0}
so that

\displaystyle\phi\geq\phi_{0}\cdot\epsilon/\tilde{O}(N^{O(\epsilon)})

Thus, if our graph is
(\leq h_{0},s_{0})
-length
\phi_{0}
-expanding and we apply
Lemma

13.4
with these parameters then we get back a
(\leq h,s)
-length
\phi
-expansion witness.

Next, apply our algorithm for computing length-constrained expander decompositions using
\epsilon_{0}
to compute a
(\leq h_{0},s_{0})
-length
\phi_{0}
-expander decomposition. Since
h_{0}\geq h
and
\epsilon_{0}=\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon)
, such a decomposition is a
(\leq h,\exp(\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon)))
-length
\phi
-expander decomposition but with a multiplicative cut slack increase of
\phi_{0}/\phi=N^{O(\epsilon_{0})}/\epsilon_{0}
for a total cut slack of (assuming
\epsilon_{0}\geq\log\log N/\log N
)

\displaystyle N^{\epsilon_{0}}\cdot N^{O(\epsilon_{0})}/\epsilon_{0}=N^{O(\epsilon_{0})\log(\epsilon_{0})}/\epsilon_{0}\leq N^{O(\epsilon_{0})}

Lastly, letting
\epsilon_{0}
be smaller by an appropriate polynomial gives a length slack of
\exp(\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon))
and cut slack of
n^{\epsilon}
. Furthermore, the time to compute this expander decomposition is as described above since our parameters have only changed by a polynomial. Furthermore, if we apply
Lemma

13.4
after applying the above decomposition we get back the desired witness. Finally, the work to invoke
Lemma

13.4
with
L=N^{\epsilon}_{0}
is

\displaystyle m\cdot\tilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon_{0}}\cdot s_{0}^{O(1/\epsilon_{0})}\cdot N^{O(\epsilon_{0})}+\frac{1}{\epsilon_{0}}\cdot N^{\epsilon_{0}}\cdot N^{O(\epsilon_{0})}\cdot\operatorname{poly}(h)\right)+\frac{\tilde{O}(1)}{\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon_{0})}\sum_{i}\textnormal{{W}}_{\textnormal{ED}}(A_{i},m_{i})

\displaystyle\leq m\cdot\tilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon_{0}}\cdot s_{0}^{O(1/\epsilon_{0})}\cdot N^{O(\epsilon_{0})}+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\cdot N^{O(\epsilon_{0})}\cdot\operatorname{poly}(h)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\tilde{O}(1)}{\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon_{0})}\sum_{i}m_{i}\cdot\tilde{O}\left(N^{O(\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon_{0}))}\cdot\operatorname{poly}(h)\right)

\displaystyle\leq m\cdot\tilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon_{0}}\cdot s_{0}^{O(1/\epsilon_{0})}\cdot N^{O(\epsilon_{0})}+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\cdot N^{O(\epsilon_{0})}\cdot\operatorname{poly}(h)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\tilde{O}(1)}{\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon_{0})}\frac{1}{\epsilon_{0}}\cdot\tilde{O}(m+n^{1+O(\epsilon_{0})}+N^{O(\epsilon_{0})})\cdot\tilde{O}\left(N^{O(\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon_{0}))}\cdot\operatorname{poly}(h)\right)

\displaystyle\leq m\cdot\tilde{O}\left(N^{O(\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon))}\cdot\operatorname{poly}(h)\right)

where above we applied the previously described algorithm for expander decompositions and in the last line we again assumed
\epsilon\geq 1/\log^{1/c}N
for a suitable large constant
c
. Note that the above is asymptotically the same as the time to compute our expander decompositions. The depth calculation is analogous.
∎

In
Section

16
we show how to achieve the above algorithm with the linkedness property. Likewise, we note that the above algorithm immediately gives “witnessed” expander decompositions. Specifically, if we run the above algorithm after already applying our expander decomposition, then we find no sparse cuts and the output of our algorithm (and, in particular,
Lemma

13.4
) is a collection of routers, one for each cluster of our neighborhood cover, along with an embedding of a router into each cluster of the neighborhood cover. We also note that plugging the above result into
Lemma

13.4
gives efficient approximation algorithms for the demand-size-largest cut problem.

16
Maintaining Extra Properties (
Theorem

1.1
with Linkedness)

In the previous sections we showed how repeatedly cutting large length-constrained sparse cuts gives length-constrained expander decompositions. In this section, we discuss how to incorporate additional graph properties—in particular, the linkedness property—into these decompositions.

Techniques.

The basic idea for achieving said properties is to use the robustness of length-constrained expanders. In particular, start with a length-constrained expander decomposition. The resulting graph is a length-constrained expander but may not have our desired properties. We then force our graph to have our properties, possibly at the expense of length-constrained expansion. By the robustness properties of length-constrained expanders, we can easily restore length-constrained expansion, now possibly at the cost of our properties. Going back and forth between restoring our properties and length-constrained expansion eventually gives us a length-constrained expander with our desired properties.

We call a
{\mathcal{P}}
set of (possibly infinitely many) graphs a
property
. We say that a graph
G
has property
{\mathcal{P}}
iff
G\in{\mathcal{P}}
.
We say that an algorithm
\mathcal{A}

maintains property
{\mathcal{P}}
with initial rate
I:\mathbb{R}^{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
and maintenance rate
M:\mathbb{R}^{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
, iff for every graph
G
on
n
vertices,

•

the algorithm
\mathcal{A}
can first find a subset
E^{\prime}\subseteq E(G)
of at most
I(n)\cdot|E(G)|
edges such that the graph
G^{\prime}=G\setminus E^{\prime}
has property
{\mathcal{P}}
; and

•

upon any online sequence of
k
edge deletions from
G^{\prime}
, the algorithm
\mathcal{A}
maintains a pruned set of at most
M(n)\cdot k
edges of
G^{\prime}
, such that when the edges in the pruned set are removed from
G^{\prime}
, the remaining graph has property
{\mathcal{P}}
.

In this case, we also say that the property
{\mathcal{P}}
has (or can be maintained with) rate
(I(\cdot),M(\cdot))
.

The main result in this section is the following theorem.
We will first prove this theorem, and then use it to achieve an additional linkedness property in
Theorem

1.1
.

Theorem 16.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{P}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{P}}_{k}
be graph properties with rates
(I_{1},M_{1}),\ldots,(I_{k},M_{k})
respectively where
M_{1}\geq\cdots\geq M_{k}
.
Assume further that

•

M_{1}\cdot\sum_{2\leq i\leq k}M_{i}<1/4
; and

•

\sum_{2\leq i\leq k}M_{i}<1/2
.

Then property
{\mathcal{P}}=\bigcap_{1\leq i\leq k}{\mathcal{P}}_{i}
can be maintained with rates
(O\big(\sum_{1\leq i\leq k}I_{i}),O(\sum_{1\leq i\leq k}M_{i}))
.

16.1
Proof of
Theorem

16.1

The remainder of this section is dedicated to the proof of
Theorem

16.1
.
We first prove the following lemma, which is the special case of
Theorem

16.1
where
k=2
.

Lemma 16.2
.

Let
{\mathcal{P}}_{1},{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
be graph properties with rates
(I_{1},M_{1})
and
(I_{2},M_{2})
.
If
M_{1}\cdot M_{2}<1/2
, then the property
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}\cap{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
can be maintained with rate
(I_{1}+(2+2M_{1})\cdot I_{2},M_{1}+2(1+M_{1})^{2}\cdot M_{2})
.

Proof.

Denote by
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
the algorithms for maintaining
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
with rates
(I_{1},M_{1})
and
(I_{2},M_{2})
, respectively.
We now describe an algorithm that maintains property
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}\cap{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
.
We first apply
\mathcal{A}_{1}
to
G
and compute a set
E^{1}_{0}\subseteq E(G)
of edges such that
|E^{1}_{0}|\leq I_{1}\cdot|E(G)|
and
G\setminus E^{1}_{0}
has property
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}
.
We then apply
\mathcal{A}_{2}
to
G\setminus E^{1}_{0}
and compute a set
E^{2}_{0}\subseteq E(G\setminus E^{1}_{0})
of edges such that
|E^{2}_{0}|\leq I_{1}\cdot|E(G\setminus(E^{1}_{0}\cup E^{2}_{0}))|
and
G\setminus(E^{1}_{0}\cup E^{2}_{0})
has property
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
.
We then perform iterations. In the
i
th iteration, we iteratively maintain properties
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
, starting with a graph
G_{i-1}
(where
G_{0}=G\setminus(E^{1}_{0}\cup E^{2}_{0})
). We apply
\mathcal{A}_{1}
to compute a set
E^{1}_{i}\subseteq E(G_{i-1})
such that
|E^{1}_{i}|\leq M_{1}\cdot|E^{2}_{i-1}|

G_{i-1}\setminus E^{1}_{i}
has property
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}
, and then apply
\mathcal{A}_{2}
to compute a set
E^{2}_{i}\subseteq E(G_{i-1}\setminus E^{1}_{i})
such that
G_{i-1}\setminus(E^{1}_{i}\cup E^{2}_{i})
has property
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}
. Define
G_{i}=G_{i-1}\setminus(E^{1}_{i}\cup E^{2}_{i})
and continue to the next iteration. Whenever in some iteration
i^{*}
we have
E^{1}_{i^{*}}=E^{2}_{i^{*}}=\emptyset
, we terminate the algorithm and return the graph
G_{i}
. In other words, the set of edges that we have removed from
G
is
E^{\prime}=\bigcup_{0\leq i\leq i^{*}}(E^{1}_{i}\cup E^{2}_{i})
.

We now describe an ball-moving abstract process and use it to estimate the size of set
E^{\prime}
. We have two boxes denoted by
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
, respectively. Initially,
B_{1}
contains
I_{1}\cdot|E(G)|

inactive
balls and
B_{2}
contains
I_{2}\cdot|E(G)|

active
balls. In each iteration, we either deactivate
t
balls in
B_{1}
(if
B_{1}
contains at least
t
active balls at the moment) and add
t\cdot M_{2}
new active balls to
B_{2}
, or deactivate
t
balls from
B_{2}
(if
B_{2}
contains at least
t
active balls at the moment) and add
t\cdot M_{1}
new active balls to
B_{1}
, for an arbitrary
t
. The process can continue for arbitrarily many iterations and may terminate at any point.

On the one hand, we show our algorithm can be modelled a realization of the process, such that the size of
E^{\prime}
is bounded by the number of balls in
B_{1}\cup B_{2}
at the terminating point.
Recall that our algorithm starts by computing a set
E_{0}^{1}
and then a set
E_{0}^{2}
to initiate properties
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
, where
|E_{0}^{1}|\leq I_{1}\cdot|E(G)|
and
|E_{0}^{2}|\leq I_{2}\cdot|E(G)|
.
The reason that the initial balls in
B_{1}
are inactive while the initial balls in
B_{2}
are active is because the set
E_{0}^{2}
is computed in graph
G\setminus E^{0}_{1}
, which means that the edges in
E^{0}_{1}
are not viewed as the online updates for property
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
.
In the first iteration after the initialization, we compute a new prune set
|E^{1}_{1}|\leq M_{1}\cdot|E_{0}^{2}|
to maintain
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}
. This can be viewed as deactivating
|E_{1}^{1}|/M_{1}\leq|E_{0}^{2}|
balls in
B_{2}
and add
|E_{1}^{1}|
new active balls in
B_{1}
.
Similarly, in the algorithm we then compute a new prune set
E^{2}_{1}
to maintain
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}
, and this can be viewed as deactivating
|E_{1}^{2}|/M_{1}
balls in
B_{2}
and add
|E_{1}^{2}|
new active balls in
B_{2}
. Later iterations can be simulated in a similar way. The algorithm ends whenever
E^{1}_{i^{*}}=E^{2}_{i^{*}}=\emptyset
for some
i^{*}
, and we let the process end at the same time.

From the definition of maintenance rate, upon
k
online updates, property
{\mathcal{P}}_{i}
can be maintained by a pruned set of at most
M_{i}\cdot k
edges. This is exactly modelled by our process. In particular, every time we compute a set
E_{i}^{1}
, in our process we deactivate
|E_{i}^{1}|/M_{2}
balls from
B_{2}
, so we are guaranteed that
r
active balls in one
B_{2}
can give birth to at most
M_{2}\cdot r
new balls in
B_{1}
. Therefore, at any time, we are never required to deactivate more balls than the current active balls in a box. As every edge in the pruned set
E^{\prime}
corresponds to a distinct ball in
B_{1}
or
B_{2}
, the size of
E^{\prime}
is bounded by the number of balls in
B_{1}\cup B_{2}
at the end of the process.

On the other hand, we show that, no matter how the process proceeds, the number of balls in
B_{1}\cup B_{2}
at any time is at most
(I_{1}+(2+2M_{1})\cdot I_{2})
. In fact, for each initial active ball in
B_{2}
, it will eventually gives birth to at most
1+M_{1}+M_{2}M_{1}+M_{1}M_{2}M_{1}+\cdots
balls in the final set. Therefore, the number of balls in
B_{1}\cup B_{2}
at any time is bounded by

\begin{split}&|E(G)|\cdot\bigg(I_{1}+I_{2}+M_{1}I_{2}+M_{2}(M_{1}I_{2})+M_{1}(M_{2}M_{1}I_{2})+\cdots\bigg)\\
&\leq|E(G)|\cdot\bigg(I_{1}+I_{2}\cdot(1+M_{1})\cdot\frac{1}{1-M_{1}M_{2}}\bigg)\\
&\leq|E(G)|\cdot(I_{1}+(2+2M_{1})\cdot I_{2}),\end{split}

as
M_{1}M_{2}\leq 1/2
. Therefore, our algorithm initializes property
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}\cap{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
with rate
I\leq I_{1}+(2+2M_{1})\cdot I_{2}
.

Now upon an update, we perform iterations as exactly described before to maintain properties
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
. Via a similar ball-moving process, we can show that the pruned set upon every update has size at most

\begin{split}M&\leq M_{1}+(1+M_{1})M_{2}+M_{1}(1+M_{1})M_{2}+M_{2}(M_{1}(1+M_{1})M_{2})+\cdots\\
&\leq M_{1}+M_{2}\cdot(1+M_{1})^{2}\cdot\frac{1}{1-M_{1}M_{2}}\\
&\leq M_{1}+2(1+M_{1})^{2}\cdot M_{2}.\end{split}

∎

We now prove
Theorem

16.1
using
Lemma

16.2
. Denote
I=\sum_{1\leq i\leq k}I_{i}
and
M=\sum_{1\leq i\leq k}M_{i}
.

We first show that property
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}=\bigcap_{2\leq i\leq k}{\mathcal{P}}_{i}
can be maintained with initial rate
I^{\prime}=O(\sum_{2\leq i\leq k}I_{i})
and maintenance rate
M^{\prime}=O(\sum_{2\leq i\leq k}M_{i})
.
We describe an algorithm similar to (but simpler than) the one in the proof of
Lemma

16.2
that maintains property
{\mathcal{P}}
. Denote by
\mathcal{A}_{i}
the algorithm for maintaining property
{\mathcal{P}}_{i}
. We perform iterations. In the first iteration, we start with a graph
G_{0}=G
, and apply
\mathcal{A}_{i}
to compute a set
E^{1}_{i}\subseteq E(G)
such that
G\setminus E^{1}_{i}
has property
{\mathcal{P}}_{i}
. Define
E^{1}=\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq k}E^{1}_{i}
, so
|E^{1}|\leq\big(\sum_{2\leq i\leq k}I_{i}\big)\cdot|E(G)|
. We remove edges in
E^{1}
from graph
G_{0}
and denote by
G_{1}
the remaining graph as the outcome of iteration
1
.
We now describe the second iteration.
Observe that, for each
1\leq i\leq k
, the graph
G_{1}
produced by iteration
1
can be seen as obtained from
G\setminus E^{1}_{i}
by removing edges in
E^{1}\setminus E^{1}_{i}
. Note that
G\setminus E^{1}_{i}
has property
{\mathcal{P}}_{i}
. From the definition of maintenance rate, we can apply
\mathcal{A}_{i}
to compute another set
E^{2}_{i}\subseteq E(G_{1})
with
|E^{2}_{i}|\leq M_{i}\cdot|E^{1}\setminus E^{1}_{i}|\leq M_{i}\cdot|E^{1}|
, such that
G_{1}\setminus E^{2}_{i}
has property
{\mathcal{P}}_{i}
.
Define
E^{2}=\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq k}E^{2}_{i}
, so
|E^{2}|\leq\big(\sum_{2\leq i\leq k}M_{i}\big)\cdot|E^{1}|
.
We sequentially perform iteration
j
for
j=3,4,\ldots
similarly and compute sets
E^{3},E^{4},\ldots
.
Whenever for some iteration the set
E^{i^{*}}
is empty, we terminate the algorithm and return
E^{\prime}
as the union of all sets
E^{1},E^{2},\ldots,E^{i^{*}}
computed in these iterations.
Via a similar ball-moving process as in the proof of
Lemma

16.2
, we can show that

\begin{split}|E^{\prime}|\leq&\sum_{2\leq i\leq k}I_{i}\cdot|E(G)|+\sum_{j\geq 1}\bigg(\sum_{2\leq i\leq k}M_{i}\bigg)^{j}\cdot\sum_{2\leq i\leq k}I_{i}\cdot|E(G)|\\
\leq&\frac{\sum_{2\leq i\leq k}I_{i}}{1-\sum_{2\leq i\leq k}M_{i}}\cdot|E(G)|\leq 2\cdot\sum_{2\leq i\leq k}I_{i}\cdot|E(G)|.\end{split}

In other words, the initial rate of property
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
is at most
I^{\prime}\leq 2\cdot\sum_{2\leq i\leq k}I_{i}
. The same algorithm can also be used to deal with online update sequence. Via similar arguments, we can show that the maintenance rate is at most

M^{\prime}=\bigg(\sum_{2\leq i\leq k}M_{i}\bigg)+\bigg(\sum_{2\leq i\leq k}M_{i}\bigg)^{2}+\bigg(\sum_{2\leq i\leq k}M_{i}\bigg)^{3}+\cdots\leq\frac{\sum_{2\leq i\leq k}M_{i}}{1-\sum_{2\leq i\leq k}M_{i}}\leq 2\cdot\sum_{2\leq i\leq k}M_{i}.

We then show that property
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}\cap{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
can be maintained with rate
(O(I),O(M))
.
Note that
M_{1}\cdot M^{\prime}\leq M_{1}\cdot 2\cdot\sum_{2\leq i\leq k}M_{i}\leq 1/2
.
Applying
Lemma

16.2
to properties
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
and
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}
, we get that property
{\mathcal{P}}
can be maintained with initial rate

I^{\prime}+(2+2M^{\prime})\cdot I_{1}\leq I^{\prime}+(2+2\cdot 2\cdot 1/2)I_{1}\leq 5\cdot\sum_{1\leq i\leq k}I_{i},

and maintenance rate

M^{\prime}+2(1+M^{\prime})^{2}\cdot M_{1}\leq M^{\prime}+2(1+2\cdot 1/2)^{2}M_{1}\leq 9\cdot\sum_{1\leq i\leq k}M_{i}.

16.2
Achieving Linkedness in
Theorem

1.1

Let
G
be the input graph. We define the property
{\mathcal{P}}(G,\beta)
as all graphs
G^{\prime}
with
G^{\prime}=G-C+L_{C}^{\beta}
for some moving cut
C
.
Clearly, for graph
G
, its initial rate is
0
, and its maintenance rate is
\beta
(as removing an edge will cause
\beta
self-loops to be added).

Set
s=\exp(\operatorname{poly}(1/\epsilon))
as in
Theorem

1.1
.
Let
{\mathcal{P}}(h,s,\phi)
be the set of all
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-expanders.

The algorithm for preserving both properties
{\mathcal{P}}(G,\beta)
and
{\mathcal{P}}(h,s,\phi)
works as follows.
Let

k=\frac{\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon)}{\epsilon},\quad k^{\prime}=1/\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon).

We first apply a
(hk^{\prime},s/\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon))
-length
\big(\phi\cdot 2^{O(k)}\cdot N^{O(1/k^{\prime})}\cdot\log^{2}N\big)
-expander decomposition. This has initial rate
\phi\cdot 2^{O(k)}\cdot N^{O(1/k^{\prime})}\cdot\log^{2}N\cdot N^{\epsilon}
.
From
Theorem

8.1
and
Theorem

11.1
, its maintenance rate is
\phi\cdot\big(N^{\epsilon}\cdot 2^{O(k)}\cdot N^{O(1/k^{\prime})}\big)\log N=\phi\cdot N^{\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon)}
.
Therefore, as long as
\beta\leq\frac{1}{2\phi}\cdot N^{\operatorname{poly}(\epsilon)}
, from
Lemma

16.2
, properties
{\mathcal{P}}(G,\beta)
and
{\mathcal{P}}(h,s,\phi)
can be achieved by alternately applying the algorithms for maintaining
{\mathcal{P}}(G,\beta)
and
{\mathcal{P}}(h,s,\phi)
, with the running time dominated by the initialization running time.
